## contrast/letting-the-agents-in-earliest-session.md

---
title: "Letting the Agents In: a Constructive Take on AI and Open Source"
slug: letting-the-agents-in
date: 2026-06-03
description: "Open source is drowning in AI-generated pull requests, and the standard response is to close the doors. I think that fights the wrong battle. If you design the contribution surface as a contract an agent can check itself against, you can let agents extend your code instead of locking them out, and stop caring who wrote the patch."
---

```DISCLAIMER: The views and opinions expressed in this blog are solely my own and do not reflect those of my employer, or any of its affiliates.```


> **TL;DR;** Open source is being flooded with AI-generated pull requests, some merely low-quality, some openly sketchy, and the standard response is to close the doors: ban AI, demand proof you are human, shut the project down. I think that fights the wrong battle. The flood hurts because contribution got cheap while *reviewing* contributions stayed expensive. So make review cheap too, on your own terms: design the contribution surface as a contract an agent can check itself against before it ever opens a pull request. Then it stops mattering whether a human or an agent wrote the patch. What you trust is the contribution, because you can check it. I have been building a small health tool this way, and it changed how I think about who, or what, gets to extend my code.

<!-- DRAFT: opening only (Path A). Rest of the post to follow: the doctrine (guide, don't enumerate) + the grammar; Verifier's Law as the lens; the worked example (teaser); the two trust tiers + Ghostty; agents propose / humans amend; the hard parts; close. -->

(Bias up front: I am an AI researcher, my PhD is in reinforcement learning and theory of mind for human-machine teams[^thesis], and I have spent years on the question of trust between people and machines. So I come at "can you trust an agent's contribution" from the trust side, not the security-vendor side. Calibrate accordingly.)

## A contributor who wasn't

In February 2026, an account called Kai Gritun opened 103 pull requests across 95 repositories in about two weeks, and got 23 of them merged into real projects, some of them infrastructure a lot of people quietly depend on.[^kai] The patches were plausible. The account behaved like an eager newcomer. It was an autonomous AI agent, and it never said so on GitHub; it admitted it only afterward, in a cold email to a maintainer whose project it had already gotten code into. The goal was not to improve anything. It was to accumulate the appearance of a trusted contributor and spend it promoting a paid service. Researchers compared the shape of it to the xz-utils backdoor: build credibility under a false identity, then cash it in.[^kai]

Kai Gritun is the sharp end of something every maintainer has felt this year, and it comes in three escalating flavors.

First, slop. Generating a confident, wrong patch is now free, and maintainers are buried. tldraw began auto-closing every external pull request.[^tldraw] The Jazzband collective, which stewarded 84 Python projects, shut down, its maintainer pointing at the flood of AI-generated spam.[^jazzband]

Second, the triage tax. Even well-meant AI reports cost real hours to disprove. Daniel Stenberg ended curl's six-year bug-bounty program after the share of valid security reports fell from roughly one in six to about one in thirty, with him spending ten to fifteen hours a week refuting fabricated vulnerabilities that a model had dressed up to look real.[^curl]

Third, the sketchy end, where Kai Gritun lives: contributions that are not noise but theater, with provenance built to deceive.

## Every answer so far is a door closing

Line up the responses and the pattern is hard to miss. Ban AI-generated code (GNOME, Flathub). Shut the project down (Jazzband). End the bounty (curl). Or, my favorite because it says the quiet part out loud, Ghostty now asks every first-time contributor to write a short note explaining themselves "in your own words, not written by AI" before a pull request is accepted.[^ghostty] The frontier of open-source defense has quietly become proving that you are a human being.

I want to steelman that reflex before I argue with it, because it comes from a fair place. Maintainers are unpaid, the volume is unsustainable, and a project that burns out its one maintainer helps nobody. Closing the door buys time, and time is the scarcest thing they have. If your only goal is to survive this quarter, a wall is a rational tool.

But as a long-run answer I think it fights the wrong battle, and my reason comes from the trust side of my own work rather than the security side.

## What the panic is about

Open-source trust always ran on a proxy. A contributor's history, their merge count, the simple fact that they seemed to be a person, all stood in for the thing you cared about: do they know what they are doing, and do they mean well. The proxy worked because it was *expensive to build*. A reputation took years, and a human who abused one had something to lose. Skin in the game is what made the proxy worth trusting.

Agents removed the cost. An agent can wear a thousand identities, has no reputation it fears losing, and faces no future you can threaten. So "is this contributor human" is a stand-in for "does this contributor have anything at stake," and for an agent the honest answer is no, and it will stay no. That is why authenticating humanness is a treadmill: you are trying to re-secure a proxy that the technology has permanently cheapened.

So I do not reach for a better bouncer. I change the question. Stop asking who wrote the patch and ask whether you can check it. Everyone is racing to keep AI out of their codebase; the more interesting move is to let it in, on a contract. What you end up trusting is the contribution, because it is verifiable, and who authored it stops being the load-bearing question.

The rest of this post is how you build that contract, what I learned trying it on a health tool of my own, and the parts I do not want to wave away.

[^thesis]: My PhD is on reinforcement learning and theory of mind in language modeling. The theory-of-mind half comes back at the end of this post, because a contribution contract turns out to be a shared mental model between a human and an agent, but that is a story for its own piece.

[^kai]: Documented by the security firm Socket (February 2026): an autonomous agent operating as "Kai Gritun" opened 103 pull requests across 95 repositories and had 23 merged in roughly two weeks, disclosing its nature only in later private outreach. Socket framed it as reputation-farming to promote a paid agent service and drew the explicit parallel to the xz-utils supply-chain attack. *(verify exact figures + link before publish)*

[^tldraw]: tldraw began auto-closing external pull requests in January 2026 (GitHub issue #7695), citing AI-generated submissions with little follow-up engagement. *(verify before publish)*

[^jazzband]: The Jazzband Python collective announced its shutdown in March 2026, its maintainer citing the volume of AI-generated spam pull requests and issues as the breaking point. *(verify before publish)*

[^curl]: Daniel Stenberg ended curl's HackerOne bug-bounty program in January 2026; the valid-report rate had fallen from about 1-in-6 to roughly 1-in-30, with significant weekly time lost to triaging AI-generated false reports. *(verify exact numbers + link before publish)*

[^ghostty]: Ghostty's "Vouch" system (February 2026) requires first-time contributors to submit a request written "in your own words, not written by AI"; unvouched pull requests are auto-closed. *(verify before publish)*

## contrast/reproducability_trap-earliest-session.md

---
title: "The SOTA Trap: Why We Are Working for Big Tech for Free?"
slug: sota-trap
image: 
date: 2025-12-09
description: "Are we doing science, or are we just unpaid QA testers? How vague 'Teaser Papers' and the 'Open-Washing' agenda are paralyzing academic research."

---

```DISCALIMER:** The views and opinions expressed in this blog are solely my own and do not reflect those of my employer, or any of its affiliates. ```


> **TLDR;** Big Tech papers are increasingly citing themselves and hiding code. This isn't just "competition"—it's a Denial of Service attack on academic labs, forcing researchers to waste months reverse-engineering "advertisements" disguised as science.

# Introduction

: We aren't standing on the shoulders of giants; we are digging in their trash looking for the hyperparameters they "forgot" to publish.

Last week, I was sitting in a lab meeting, and the vibe was… tense. 

We were discussing a new paper dropped by a major tech company (you know the ones). The abstract was beautiful. The results were SOTA. The "intuition" section was poetic. But when it came to the implementation details? **Vague.**

*"We used standard hyperparameters,"* the paper claimed. 
(Narrator: They did not, in fact, use standard hyperparameters.)

As my colleagues debated how to reverse-engineer the learning rate schedule and how many thousands of GPU hours we’d need to burn just to *verify* their baseline, I had a realization that left me like: 
![surprise](https://media1.giphy.com/media/v1.Y2lkPTc5MGI3NjExaHkwM2E5ejQ2OW41ZXQ1OWl2eG80bmw3azBvNnZkbndlMzBvaXNmayZlcD12MV9pbnRlcm5hbF9naWZfYnlfaWQmY3Q9Zw/vQqeT3AYg8S5O/giphy.gif)

**We are not doing science. We are doing free Quality Assurance (QA) for Big Tech.**

We are taking their "Teaser Papers" —which are essentially advertisements for their proprietary models— and spending months of our lives trying to make them work. We debug their frameworks (PyTorch/JAX), we optimize their architectures, and we validate their claims. And what do we get in return? A citation? 

It felt like a trap. We are chasing their tail, incentivized to replicate their "Scale" results, while they hold all the keys (the data and the compute).

And once you see it, you can’t unsee it. And the data backs this up.

# The Disappearing Act: Where is Europe?

If you want to see who is winning this "Scale Game", you don't need to look further than the acceptance statistics for **NeurIPS 2025** [^aiword_credits].

[^aiword_credits]: The image is kindly given from [AI world](https://aiworld.eu/story/from-beijing-to-san-francisco-what-neurips-2025-reveals-about-ai-leadership). Check out their post to get an interactive view and compare 2024 with 2025 to see how the EU contribution shrinks! 

<img src="/assets/images/blog/reprod_trap/neruips2025_stats.png" alt="Number of publication by country in NeurIPS 2025" width="80%"/>


I was shocked to read the preliminary numbers. 
* **China** now accounts for roughly **36%** of accepted papers while the US holds the other **36%**.
* **Tsinghua University** is neck-and-neck with **Google** for the top spot.
* **US Big Tech** (Google, Meta, Microsoft) holds the fortress on the other side.



**And Europe?** Europe is being squeezed out with 15% (a 2% reduction from 2024). 

Now, you might point out that this is partly due to "Home Bias" — the tendency for researchers to cite and review their own heavily. And yes, recent studies confirm that Chinese researchers cite each other at a rate of nearly **57%**[^home_bias] (against the 37.1% from the US), creating a massive, self-reinforcing echo chamber. 

[^home_bias]: *“Paper Tiger? Chinese Science and Home Bias in Citations”* (NBER 2024). A fascinating read if you want to see the raw numbers on citation cartels vs. structural bias.

But blaming "Home Bias" misses the bigger, scarier point.

The problem isn't just that they cite each other. The problem is that the "game" of AI research has shifted entirely to **Capital-Intensive Science**. 
To get a paper into NeurIPS in 2025, you often need to run experiments that cost more than an entire university department's annual budget.

### The "Compute" Fallacy

People often look at this and say, *"Oh, Europe just needs more GPUs! That’s why we have the [AI Factories](/news/ai-factories/)!"*

But I’m starting to think it’s not *only* about hardware. Yes, we need the compute, but the US Big Tech companies aren't just winning because they have H100s. They are winning because they have created a **closed loop of "Open-Washing."**

They publish papers that *look* open but are actually **insular**. A new study on "Industry Insularity" found that Big Tech papers increasingly cite *only* other industry papers[^insularity]. They are building a walled garden of knowledge that references itself to validate itself.

[^insularity]: *“Big Tech-Funded AI Papers Have Higher Citation Impact, Greater Insularity, and Larger Recency Bias”* (arXiv, Dec 2024). Basically, Big Tech likes to talk to itself.


Europe is trying to play "fair" in a game where the other two players are either:
1.  **Flooding the zone** with volume (China).
2.  **Privatizing the recipe** while publicizing the result (US Big Tech).

We are stuck in the middle, wasting our limited resources trying to replicate results that might not even be reproducible in principle.

## contrast/writing-for-machines-earliest-session.md

---
title: "Writing for Machines"
slug: writing-for-machines
date: 2026-07-14
published: false
description: "People are evolving a separate way of writing instructions for AI agents, and I spent a year measuring how it differs from how we write for humans. The register is real and measurable. Whether it is the right way to write is a question the same measurements refuse to answer."
---

`DISCLAIMER: The views and opinions expressed in this blog are solely my own and do not reflect those of my employer, or any of its affiliates.`

> **TL;DR;** A separate way of writing has appeared in the instructions people hand to AI agents: harder, terser, more commanding than the way we write for humans. I can measure exactly how it differs. What I cannot do, with the same data, is tell you it is the better way to write.

<!-- DRAFT STATE: steps 1-3 of the Path drafted (cold open, provenance/paper-death, N-RQ5). Steps 4-8 (confound, structure, adoption turn, convergence thesis, close) still to write. Numbers verified against _drafts/timbro_paper_blogpost_map.md; re-pull from paper/code/ws3 before publish. -->

## The same instruction, written for humans and for machines

Here are two ways to tell someone how to run a project's test suite.

The first is the sort of thing you find in a README, written for a person who has just shown up:

> Once the dependencies are installed you can run the tests with `pytest`. Most contributors run the fast subset while they work and save the full suite for just before opening a pull request.

The second is the sort of thing that shows up in a `SKILL.md`, one of the files people now write to instruct an AI coding agent:

> Run `pytest -q`. If it fails, read the first failure, fix the root cause, and run it again. Do not continue until the suite is green.

Same task, and roughly the same information. The voice is not the same. The second version is shorter and more clipped, and it gives orders. It does not hedge, it does not explain itself to a newcomer, and it assumes a reader who will do what the words say and nothing they merely imply.

That gap is the subject of this post. A README and a `SKILL.md` are both documentation, but they are aimed at two different readers. The README is written for a person deciding whether to use your project: someone who skims, forms a quick impression, and leaves if it looks like work. The `SKILL.md` is written for a machine that will execute the instructions literally, never skim, and never forgive a sentence with room left in it. Over the past two years people have started writing differently for that second reader, and I wanted to know whether the difference is real in the text itself (something you can measure) or whether it is just a feeling.

It is real. Before I get to how large it is, a word on where the numbers come from, and why they are in a blog post to begin with. 

## Why it is not a paper

This started as a paper. I built a corpus of a bit over 672,000 `SKILL.md` files, together with human-written READMEs to compare them against, and measured all of it. It is a blog post rather than a paper for a bitter reason. Out of everything I found, exactly one result is confirmatory, everything else is a lead. Some of the leads are surprising, and I will show them, but a lead is not a finding[^lead], and I have argued before that dressing weak results up as strong ones is most of what is wrong with how we publish[^sota]. So as I go, I will mark which is which.

## The register is real, and you can measure it

On every measure I looked at, instructions written for agents differ from instructions written for humans, and they differ in the same direction across all of them.

Each measure is deliberately simple: a rule a program applies the same way every time, with no model and no judgment in the loop. That is the point of building them this way, because it means anyone can rerun the exact computation and get the same number back. Here they are: how many of the sentences are commands (imperative mood), how much the writing hedges[^hedge] (words like _might_, _could_, _probably_), the reading-grade level, how deeply the sentences nest grammatically, and how much neighbouring sentences reuse the same words (a rough proxy for how tightly the text hangs together). Five numbers, and the same document always produces the same five.

[The comparison that matters holds the calendar fixed.[^bracket] ](this sentence is ont understandable, what are we trying to say?) If you simply put old human READMEs next to new agent files, you measure how writing drifted over the years as much as who it was written for. So the honest contrast puts human docs and agent docs from the same stretch of time side by side, both after 2023. On that contrast all five measures separate, and the largest gap is in readability:

| Measure                        | For agents, the writing is | Effect size (Cohen's d) |
| ------------------------------ | -------------------------- | ----------------------- |
| Reading grade (Flesch-Kincaid) | about 4 grades harder      | 0.40                    |
| Sentence depth                 | more deeply nested         | 0.26                    |
| Commands (imperative)          | roughly 50% more of them   | 0.25                    |
| Hedging\*                      | about half as much         | 0.09                    |
| Word-reuse cohesion            | slightly tighter           | 0.06                    |

_Every measure separates agent-directed from human-directed writing, and all in the same direction. Effect sizes are Cohen's d[^d]. N = 242,159 documents, all post-2023._

Take the readability number first, because it is the easiest to feel. Agent instructions land about four grade levels higher on the Flesch-Kincaid scale than human instructions from the same period. Four grade levels is roughly the jump from a general-audience newspaper to a dense university handout. This means that people write harder, denser prose when the reader is a machine. The commands go up by about half, the sentences nest deeper, and the hedging falls. If you have written one of these files, none of this will surprise you: you were writing for something that does not get bored and does not need to be eased in, so you cut the throat-clearing and started giving orders.

One of those five rows carries an asterisk. Four of the gaps have a deflating explanation waiting for them: a lot of these agent files were written by AI in the first place, so maybe I am measuring how machines write, not how people write for machines. The hedging row is the one that refuses that explanation, and it refuses it by pointing the opposite way from what I expected. That is the thread I pull on next.

## How much of this is just AI writing?

A large share of these `SKILL.md` files were not written by people. They were written by AI, since asking a model to draft the instructions for another model is the easy way to produce one. If agent files read harder and more commanding, maybe no human adapted anything, and I am only measuring how models write when left to themselves.

So I checked. I took several hundred files I could confidently attribute to a model and asked whether they lean the same way as the wider agent corpus. On four of the five measures they do. The reading grade, the sentence depth, the imperative commands, and the cohesion all push in the same direction whether a person or a model wrote the file. That is bad news for reading those four as evidence of humans adapting, and I will not lean on them for that claim.

The fifth measure does something I did not expect. Hedging is the one axis where the model-written files break the pattern, and they break it backwards: the model-written files hedge more, not less. A model trained to be cautious and to cover itself sprinkles in the _mights_ and the _coulds_. The human-written agent files are the ones that strip them out. So the drop in hedging cannot be an artifact of machines drafting the docs, because when machines draft the docs the hedging goes up. Someone is cutting the hedges deliberately, and it is the humans, once the reader is a machine.

That is the one thread here that clearly points at people changing how they write rather than models producing the text. It is the smallest of the five effects. It is also the cleanest, and it is why I think there is a real story in this and not only a portrait of AI's own style. It also sent me looking somewhere I had not measured yet: not the words at all, but the shape of the document.

## The bigger tell is the shape, not the words 

When I stopped measuring the words and started measuring the layout, the gaps got larger.

A `SKILL.md` and a README differ in more than diction. They sit differently on the page: how much of the text is bullet lists, how many section headings, how much fenced code, how many links out to other pages. I measured thirteen of these structural features the same mechanical way as the rest. Eight of the thirteen show a wider agent-versus-human gap than the largest of the five word-level gaps. The shape of the document carries more of the signal than the vocabulary does.

The direction fits the reader. Agent files lean heavily on sections, bullets, and code blocks, and go light on links. A link is an invitation to leave the page and come back, a human move; an agent working through a task does not browse the web for context mid-step. A deep outline with fenced commands is what you write for a reader that executes one line at a time.

I ran the same authorship check here, and it splits the result in a way worth stating plainly. Part of the structural gap is a drafting habit models have: they  love bullet lists and bold text, and that bullet-and-bold density does show up in the model-written files too. The part that does not reproduce when a model writes the file is the deep sectioning and the heavy use of code. Human-written agent docs are more deeply structured than anything the models produce on their own. So the layout signal comes in two pieces: some of it is models writing like models, and some of it is people rebuilding a document for a reader that executes. The second piece is the interesting one, and it holds up.

By now I had a register I could measure and, in two places, tie to human hands. The obvious next question is whether any of it is _good_.

## Does any of it predict which skills people use?

Here the story stops cooperating.

If a machine register exists, is there a right way to write in it? Everyone who maintains a `SKILL.md` wants the same recipe: write it this way and agents will use your skill, write it that way and they will not. I had the data to look, because alongside the text I had adoption numbers for a labelled slice of the corpus, a record of how often each skill was installed.

The answer is thin, and where it is not thin it points the wrong way. Of the five word-level measures, exactly one has any durable link to installs: imperative density. More commands, a little more adoption. The other four predict nothing about whether a skill gets used. And when I tested the structural features, the ones carrying most of the register, the effects ran against the obvious prior. The intuition that clean, scannable formatting wins turns out backwards here: the more bullet-heavy and code-heavy a skill's file, the _less_ it was installed. The one structural feature that went with more installs was links, the feature agent docs use least.

I want to be careful about the weight this carries.  The skills I have install counts for are a small and unrepresentative slice, under two percent of the corpus, gathered from one index and skewed toward one platform. So read this as "no clean signal in the best data I have", not as "proven that style does not matter". But the direction is the opposite of the recipe everyone wants, and that is worth sitting with.

The one signal that did survive, imperative density, I tried to confirm on a fresh batch of newer skills, the way you would validate any instrument before trusting it.[^klartext] It did not hold. On the newer cohort the effect flipped sign, and the model I had trained to rank skills by their text ranked them slightly worse than a coin flip. That newer batch is drifted and biased toward already-popular skills, so I read it as a failure to replicate on this cohort rather than proof the first result was noise. Either way the honest report is the same: I do not have a rule you can trust.

So I am left with a strange pair of facts. The register is real and measurable. Nothing I can measure says it is the right way to write.

## A dialect spreading by imitation, not by evidence

Put the two halves together and you get the thing I believe. People are converging on a way of writing for machines, and they are converging because they copy each other, not because anyone has shown it works.

The copying is not a figure of speech. When I clustered the corpus looking for distinct house styles, the tightest clusters were not communities of taste. They were template farms: single repositories that stamp out thousands of near-identical skill files by swapping a noun here and there. Twenty-three thousand documents sat in nine such farms. The register spreads the way conventions always spread online, by imitation of whatever the last popular example did, and the paper trail of that imitation is sitting in the duplicates.

This is where the two readers from the opening come back. A `SKILL.md` is written for a machine that executes it, and it is adopted by a human who reads it and decides to install it. Writers are tuning the prose for the first reader: harder, terser, more commanding, shaped for something that runs the steps. The evidence I have says adoption is decided by the second reader, and the traits people are tuning either do not matter to that decision or work against it. We are polishing a document for the reader that executes it while the reader that adopts it holds the veto. No wonder the two signals come apart.

I have built enough agent systems to be wary of exactly this move: adopting a practice because it looks right and everyone else does it, with no external check that it works.[^klartext] A register that spreads by imitation is not automatically wrong. It might turn out to be right. For now it is a convention wearing the costume of a best practice, and those are different things.

## What would earn a style guide

I am not going to hand you a style guide, because the state of the evidence does not support one. I can say what would earn one.

It would take the experiment I did not run.[^rq3] Hold a skill's content fixed, rewrite only its register, and measure whether the agent completes the task more often. That is the test that separates a real effect from a convention. Adoption numbers cannot do it, because adoption is a human decision tangled up with popularity and platform and luck. Task success on held-constant content can.

Until someone runs that, here is where I would leave anyone writing these files today. The register is real, so you are not imagining that a `SKILL.md` should sound different from a README. Write for the reader that executes: drop the throat-clearing, give direct instructions, lay the document out so a machine can walk it top to bottom. But hold the confidence loosely. The harder, denser, more commanding style is what everyone is converging on, and "everyone is converging on it" is the one thing I can prove and the one thing that, by itself, means nothing.

We started writing for a reader that never skims and never forgives a vague sentence, and it shows. Whether we write for it well, we have not yet answered.

[^lead]: A finding is a result I have stress-tested: I wrote the prediction down before looking, ran the strict version of the test, and it held, so I am willing to stake a claim on it. A lead is a pattern I noticed that has not cleared that bar, so it might be real or it might be an artifact of how I looked. Fields fill up with trouble when leads get reported as findings.

[^d]: Cohen's d measures a gap in standard deviations, so it does not depend on the units of any one measure and the five stay comparable. As a rough guide, 0.2 is a small effect, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large. The readability gap at 0.40 is small-to-medium; the rest are smaller still.

[^hedge]: In linguistics, hedging is language that softens a claim or signals the writer is not fully committed to it: _might_, _could_, _seems_, _in some cases_, _roughly_. I count hedge terms per thousand words, so a lower number means flatter, more direct assertion.

[^bracket]: To pull audience apart from time, I compared three groups rather than two: human documentation from before 2023, human documentation from after 2023, and agent documentation from after 2023. The gap between the two human groups measures what changed with time alone; the gap between the post-2023 humans and the post-2023 agents measures what changed because the reader is a machine. This three-way split was fixed before I looked at the results.

[^sota]: [I made that argument in [The SOTA Trap](/blog/sota-trap/): a paper shipped without code and data is a press release, not a scientific contribution. Holding myself to it here means I will not promote a nice-looking correlation to a "finding".]( i like the information delivered here, but the still needs to change 20% closer to mine)

[^klartext]: I have argued that orchestrating agents well is mostly an exercise in [saying no](/blog/klartext-leichte-sprache/): no self-grading, no scores you cannot compare against a baseline. The same caution applies to how we write for them, and to trusting a text-to-installs model before it replicates.

[^rq3]: The design is frozen: restyle the prose, hold the content fixed, measure the agent's task-success rate. I have not run it. If you want to, the corpus and the measures are the reproducible part of this work.

This started as a paper. I built a corpus of a bit over 672,000 `SKILL.md` files, together with human-written READMEs to compare them against, and measured all of it. So I will keep the two clearly apart: one thing I am fairly sure of, and several things worth chasing.

## The register is real, and you can measure it

Start with the one result I trust. On every measure I looked at, instructions written for agents differ from instructions written for humans, and they differ in the same direction across all of them.

| Measure                        | For agents, the writing is | Effect size (Cohen's d) |
| ------------------------------ | -------------------------- | ----------------------- |

| Sentence depth                 | more deeply nested         | 0.26                    |
| Commands (imperative)          | roughly 50% more of them   | 0.25                    |
